- WD-IDEA-CONT-119 | CONT | planned | Within-scene shot chaining | Problem: each shot's prompt is generated independently, so if shot 1 ends with a character sitting down, shot 2 may describe them standing. ‹src: 3.7-phase-6-shot-chaining, 3.7(extra-doc-covered-in-6)WT-E-task›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-120 | CONT | voice,planned | Within-scene shot chaining | Shot visual descriptions should understand their place relative to the previous and next shots and keep continuity between them. Frame prompts should be precise about framing and the physical placement of the mise-en-scène, and keep it continuous throughout the scene unless explicitly asked to change. ‹src: Issues, 3.7-tasks-part-2(3.8), 3.7(extra-doc-covered-in-6)WT-E-task›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-121 | CONT | planned,voice | Within-scene shot chaining | Shot-to-shot prompt chaining: within a scene, each shot's frame and video prompts receive a summary of the earlier shots' key visual and narrative state, so shot 2 knows what happened in shot 1 and action stays continuous, especially in action-heavy scenes. Prompts reflect each character's current scene state. The chained context is visible, editable, and can be turned on or off per scene. Legacy docs state that chaining is part of the MVP; it is tagged planned as an unbuilt feature. ‹src: Tickets, master-mvp-tasklist, injecting-org-tickets-into-plan›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-122 | CONT | planned | Within-scene shot chaining | After each shot, a compact end-state summary is extracted. It covers character positions; character states (posture, expression, action completed); prop states (moved, broken); environmental changes (lighting shift, door opened); and the emotional beat. The summary contains no camera information. ‹src: 3.7-phase-6-shot-chaining, 3.7(extra-doc-covered-in-6)WT-E-task›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-123 | CONT | planned | Within-scene shot chaining | The prior shot's end state is injected into the next shot's prompt. Positions, expressions, and physical states continue unless the action explicitly changes them. ‹src: 3.7-phase-6-shot-chaining, 3.7(extra-doc-covered-in-6)WT-E-task›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-124 | CONT | planned | Within-scene shot chaining | Division of labor: transformation events decide which appearance description applies to each shot. Chaining carries action, position, and state continuity, supplementing those rather than replacing them, and must not re-derive appearance. ‹src: 3.7-phase-6-shot-chaining, 3.7(extra-doc-covered-in-6)WT-E-task›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-125 | CONT | planned | Within-scene shot chaining | If a transformation occurs in a shot, that shot's end state must reflect the post-transformation description (for example, "Character X has now transformed to ..."). ‹src: 3.7-phase-6-shot-chaining, 3.7(extra-doc-covered-in-6)WT-E-task›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-126 | CONT | idea | Within-scene shot chaining | A user-settable "scene opening state," such as "scene opens with all characters seated around the table," so the chain does not start weak from a vague first shot. ‹src: 3.7-phase-6-shot-chaining›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-127 | CONT | planned | Within-scene shot chaining | Each shot after the first shows a collapsible "Context from prior shot" section that the user can view and edit. The chain context is a persisted save state, giving continuity control without editing prompts. ‹src: 3.7-phase-6-shot-chaining, 3.7(extra-doc-covered-in-6)WT-E-task›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-128 | CONT | planned | Within-scene shot chaining | "Regenerate from here" re-chains from a chosen shot forward using the edited context, re-extracting end states for all later shots, with a progress indicator. ‹src: 3.7-phase-6-shot-chaining, 3.7(extra-doc-covered-in-6)WT-E-task›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-129 | CONT | planned | Within-scene shot chaining | A scene-level "Chain prompts" toggle is on by default for scenes with 2 or more shots, since a single static shot may not need it. Chain and broken-chain icons appear between shot cards, and the chain context sits below the transformation badges, with both visible. ‹src: 3.7-phase-6-shot-chaining, 3.7(extra-doc-covered-in-6)WT-E-task›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-130 | CONT | planned | Within-scene shot chaining | Chaining is inherently sequential and adds one extraction call per shot, roughly doubling prompt-generation time for a scene. Users should be warned.
- WD-IDEA-CONT-131 | CONT | planned | Within-scene shot chaining | Chaining amplifies whatever quality the prompts already have, so frame-prompt quality work should come first.
- WD-IDEA-CONT-132 | CONT | idea | Within-scene shot chaining | Each shot carries continuity flags that mark handoffs between characters, prop exchanges, and character entries into and exits from the shot, within the shot list. ‹src: architecture-and-rules, database-schema-state-transition-matrix, golden-test-evaluation-framework›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-133 | CONT | alt | Within-scene shot chaining › Options: how much prior-shot context a chained shot sees | Options for how much prior-shot context a chained shot sees. Rolling (recommended): shot N sees only shot N-1's end state, which keeps context bounded, while earlier state still carries forward through the chain. The alternative is cumulative, where each shot sees all prior end states. ‹src: 3.7-phase-6-shot-chaining, 3.7(extra-doc-covered-in-6)WT-E-task›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-134 | CONT | alt | Within-scene shot chaining › Options: how much prior-shot context a chained shot sees | Options for how much prior-shot context a chained shot sees. Cumulative: each shot sees all prior end states, or the prior summary plus an optional cumulative scene state; this is richer but risks prompt bloat. The alternative is rolling (recommended), where shot N sees only shot N-1's end state. ‹src: 3.7-phase-6-shot-chaining, 3.7(extra-doc-covered-in-6)WT-E-task›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-135 | CONT | idea | Adjacent frame continuity: seeds, match, camera change | A shot's start frame is seeded from the previous shot's end frame, even when the new shot is very different. Its prompt references that end frame and keeps character positions consistent with it. ‹src: architecture-and-rules, AI-agent-registry-context-flow-architecture, project-overview›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-136 | CONT | idea | Adjacent frame continuity: seeds, match, camera change | In a multi-shot sequence, each shot has its own frame-plus-video prompt pair. The ending composition of one shot informs the starting frame of the next, so shot 2's start frame matches the end of shot 1. ‹src: Frame_Video_Prompt_Format_Guide›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-137 | CONT | idea | Adjacent frame continuity: seeds, match, camera change | Use first/last-frame control between clips for smooth handoffs from one clip to the next. ‹src: Frame_Video_Prompt_Format_Guide›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-138 | CONT | voice | Adjacent frame continuity: seeds, match, camera change | "Match time point": one shot's end frame can seed the not-yet-generated start frame of the next shot. The creator can designate it either as matching completely (copied over) or as a "cut to different angle" generated from it. ‹src: new-tasks›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-139 | CONT | impl | Adjacent frame continuity: seeds, match, camera change | Each transition between adjacent shots is classified by comparing camera specs as match, camera change, or none. The AI recommends a mode and the user can override it. Match reuses the previous end frame exactly, camera change recomposes the previous end frame for a new angle via vision analysis, and none applies no continuity. Frames carry directional controls ("Match from", "Ref from", "Push Match to", "Push Ref to") and link status badges. ‹src: discussion-reference-continuity, 3.7-phase-5-non-adjacent-continuity, 3.8-phase5-non-adjacent-continuity›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-140 | CONT | impl | Adjacent frame continuity: seeds, match, camera change | Match mode: the start frame is a pixel-identical copy of the previous shot's end frame at zero generation cost, and its prompt describes that same visual state without introducing differences. Frame continuity links come in two types: match, as described, and reference, where vision analysis of the reference frame identifies what must be preserved versus what changes and builds an enhanced continuity prompt. ‹src: system-prompts-inventory, 3.8-phase5-non-adjacent-continuity, 3.8-threads›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-141 | CONT | impl | Adjacent frame continuity: seeds, match, camera change | Camera change: when the camera angle changes from the previous shot, a separate recomposition frame is generated from the previous end frame, which serves as the primary identity reference for the next start frame. A dedicated continuity frame prompt is generated automatically. A standalone description of the shot is still written as a fallback. ‹src: system-prompts-inventory, discussion-reference-continuity›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-142 | CONT | impl | Adjacent frame continuity: seeds, match, camera change | A recomposition re-shoots the previous end moment from a new camera position. It keeps exact facial expressions and emotional state, clothing, accessories and hair, lighting quality and color temperature, background and atmosphere, prop positions and states, and time of day and weather. Only the camera position, framing, and depth of field change. ‹src: system-prompts-inventory›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-143 | CONT | impl | Adjacent frame continuity: seeds, match, camera change | Before recomposition, the AI can analyze the actual previous end-frame image. That analysis counts as ground truth and wins over stored metadata descriptions when they disagree. ‹src: system-prompts-inventory›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-144 | CONT | voice | Adjacent frame continuity: seeds, match, camera change › Push/pull reference and camera-change fidelity | The creator wants "push/pull reference to/from" a frame, also called the camera-angle-change option. It carries the exact scene moment from one frame into another frame re-shot from a different camera angle. ‹src: discussion-reference-continuity›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-145 | CONT | impl | Adjacent frame continuity: seeds, match, camera change › Push/pull reference and camera-change fidelity | "Pull Ref" and "Push Ref" attach another frame's image as a continuity reference for the target frame's generation. The generator is told to maintain the characters and subjects from that reference. ‹src: discussion-reference-continuity›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-146 | CONT | idea | Adjacent frame continuity: seeds, match, camera change › Push/pull reference and camera-change fidelity | Identified gap: the continuity reference image goes to the generator with a prompt built from shot metadata (names, setting, camera), not from what is actually visible in the reference frame. The generator gets only generic information, such as "Character A, office," and does not know that A is mid-frown with a raised left hand. ‹src: discussion-reference-continuity›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-147 | CONT | idea | Adjacent frame continuity: seeds, match, camera change › Push/pull reference and camera-change fidelity | Fidelity expectations when re-angling from a reference image: small changes (slight zoom or reframe) work reasonably well. Medium changes (two-shot to close-up) are hit or miss, with face drift and hallucinated backgrounds. Large changes (POV, reverse angles) are unreliable because the generator must invent too much. ‹src: discussion-reference-continuity›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-148 | CONT | alt | Adjacent frame continuity: seeds, match, camera change › Push/pull reference and camera-change fidelity | Dedicated multi-angle or virtual-camera generators (novel-view and multi-angle image-edit models) were considered for angle changes. None handles all scenarios reliably. ‹src: discussion-reference-continuity›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-149 | CONT | voice,planned,impl | Adjacent frame continuity: seeds, match, camera change › Push/pull reference and camera-change fidelity | Analyze-then-generate (also called Describe-then-Generate; suggested by the creator and the research consensus): before generating a camera-change or reference-linked frame, a vision model turns the reference frame into a structured scene description. The description covers each character's position, expression, clothing, and posture; the environment by foreground, midground, and background, plus props; lighting; and color palette. It notes what must be preserved versus what changes. That description plus the new camera specs becomes the prompt. ‹src: discussion-reference-continuity, 3.8-phase5-non-adjacent-continuity, 3.8-threads›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-150 | CONT | planned | Adjacent frame continuity: seeds, match, camera change › Push/pull reference and camera-change fidelity | The camera-change prompt should be concrete and frame-specific, for example: "the blonde man in a navy suit at frame-left is frowning, hands on the table; maintain this exact appearance; now recompose from [new angle]." It should not be a generic "preserve from reference." ‹src: discussion-reference-continuity›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-151 | CONT | planned | Adjacent frame continuity: seeds, match, camera change › Push/pull reference and camera-change fidelity | Dual anchoring: the source image is kept as a visual anchor alongside the semantic scene description, rather than relying on either one alone. ‹src: discussion-reference-continuity›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-152 | CONT | idea | Adjacent frame continuity: seeds, match, camera change › Push/pull reference and camera-change fidelity | The user can manually write or override the continuity prompt for a camera-change frame. Even a very detailed manual override should still be enriched by the analysis rather than skipping it. ‹src: discussion-reference-continuity›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-153 | CONT | voice | Adjacent frame continuity: seeds, match, camera change › Push/pull reference and camera-change fidelity | The creator asks whether a shot's linked assets (characters, locations, and props defined during asset extraction and assigned to shots) should also keep camera-change frames consistent, or whether the reference image alone is enough. The creator wants the decision made for the best overall UX and fidelity. ‹src: discussion-reference-continuity›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-154 | CONT | idea | Adjacent frame continuity: seeds, match, camera change › Push/pull reference and camera-change fidelity | The previous end frame alone is not enough to hold identity. Generators cannot reliably tell similarly dressed characters apart, reproduce subtle expressions, or keep clothing details, accessories, scars, and tattoos from an image alone. ‹src: discussion-reference-continuity›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-155 | CONT | planned | Adjacent frame continuity: seeds, match, camera change › Push/pull reference and camera-change fidelity | The canonical text descriptions of a shot's assigned assets (character appearance, clothing, props) should feed the camera-change continuity prompt, not only the standard start-frame prompt. ‹src: discussion-reference-continuity›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-156 | CONT | idea | Adjacent frame continuity: seeds, match, camera change › Push/pull reference and camera-change fidelity | Continuity "trifecta": three independent sources of truth, more robust than any two. The reference image anchors composition, lighting, color, mood, and film aesthetic, preventing style drift. The scene analysis captures the frame-specific moment (positions, expressions, spatial layout), preventing hallucination. Asset descriptions supply canonical clothing and physical features, preventing character drift. ‹src: discussion-reference-continuity›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-157 | CONT | planned | Adjacent frame continuity: seeds, match, camera change › Push/pull reference and camera-change fidelity | Reference image order for camera-change frames: the previous end frame comes first because it establishes the scene moment, then character asset images reinforce identity, and style capsule references follow. ‹src: discussion-reference-continuity›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-158 | CONT | planned | Adjacent frame continuity: seeds, match, camera change › Push/pull reference and camera-change fidelity | Camera-change pipeline: analyze the source frame, gather the shot's asset descriptions, and build the prompt from the analysis, the asset descriptions, and the target camera specs (for example, "Elena (auburn hair, charcoal blazer, silver pendant) at frame-left, frowning… recompose as ECU"). Then assemble the ordered reference images and generate. ‹src: discussion-reference-continuity›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-159 | CONT | impl,planned | Adjacent frame continuity: seeds, match, camera change › Push/pull reference and camera-change fidelity | The continuity reference frame is the primary input to camera-change generation, which also draws on the prior shot's full camera and location metadata, the location's views, and character angle variants, so the recomposed frame stays spatially consistent. Regenerating the continuity prompt includes structured camera metadata, the assigned direction, selected location views, any active blocking reference, the current continuity reference frame, and the same fallback and delta wording as standard generation. Canonical location references are included when they do not conflict with the continuity frame. ‹src: 5-3-location-implementation-plan, location-continuity-progress-audit, 7-19-location-stabilization-master-plan›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-160 | CONT | idea | Adjacent frame continuity: seeds, match, camera change › Push/pull reference and camera-change fidelity | The quality gain should be invisible: the user clicks Pull Ref, or an auto-detected camera change fires, and the result is simply better, with no new buttons, modals, or decisions. ‹src: discussion-reference-continuity›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-161 | CONT | future | Adjacent frame continuity: seeds, match, camera change › Push/pull reference and camera-change fidelity | Phase 2: after generation, show a "continuity confidence" indicator, or silently log it, based on comparing the new frame with its reference. ‹src: discussion-reference-continuity›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-162 | CONT | voice,alt | Adjacent frame continuity: seeds, match, camera change › Options: tiering camera changes | Creator proposal: tier camera changes. Simple changes (zoom in/out, medium to full, two-shot to close-up) would use the input image without analysis. Complex changes would use analyze-then-generate with dual anchoring. ‹src: discussion-reference-continuity›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-163 | CONT | alt | Adjacent frame continuity: seeds, match, camera change › Options: tiering camera changes | Counter-recommendation: analyze every camera-change frame with no tiering. Even "simple" changes need to know which character to push in on, their exact expression, and what drops out of frame. Zoom-outs require inventing environment, the simple/complex line is fragile, and users will notice uneven quality if only some frames get analysis. ‹src: discussion-reference-continuity›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-164 | CONT | voice,alt | Adjacent frame continuity: seeds, match, camera change › Options: when the reference analysis runs | Option 1, at generation time (recommended): re-run the image analysis on every regeneration of a reference-linked or camera-change frame, for consistent quality and always-fresh results at a small extra cost and latency. One vision analysis costs roughly $0.01 to $0.03 and takes 2 to 3 seconds, against 10 to 30 or more seconds for the frame itself, so users are not expected to notice the latency. Analysis timing is open: at generation time (simpler, always fresh), at pull time with the result cached, or cached on the frame so regenerations do not re-analyze. The creator would switch to caching if re-analysis proves truly redundant. ‹src: discussion-reference-continuity›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-165 | CONT | alt | Adjacent frame continuity: seeds, match, camera change › Options: when the reference analysis runs | Analysis runs at pull time, and the result is cached for the generation. ‹src: discussion-reference-continuity›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-166 | CONT | voice,alt | Adjacent frame continuity: seeds, match, camera change › Options: when the reference analysis runs | Option 2: analyze once on first generation, cache the analysis on the frame, and reuse it in later regenerations. This is faster, but it goes stale if the user has edited the prompt. The creator would switch to this if re-analysis proves truly redundant. ‹src: discussion-reference-continuity›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-167 | CONT | voice,alt | Adjacent frame continuity: seeds, match, camera change › Options: what happens right after Pull Ref | Badge plus hint (chosen): the reference badge appears at once with a toast ("Reference linked — regenerate to apply the new camera angle"), and the user regenerates when ready. Unlike a match, which copies immediately, a reference only takes effect on the next regeneration, because regeneration is what spends credits. ‹src: discussion-reference-continuity›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-168 | CONT | voice,alt | Adjacent frame continuity: seeds, match, camera change › Options: what happens right after Pull Ref | Badge plus automatic regeneration: the fastest option, but it spends credits without an explicit action. The creator would switch to it if the hint proves annoying. ‹src: discussion-reference-continuity›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-169 | CONT | idea | Continuity links | Match links and reference links differ. A match link is a reactive copy: the target is identical to the source, and updates propagate. A reference link is non-reactive: the new image is inspired by the source but separate, and it never triggers propagation. ‹src: discussion-reference-continuity›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-170 | CONT | idea | Continuity links | Reference links are first-class frame-to-frame links, like match links, so they can be shown, removed cleanly, and reasoned about. Push Ref creates the same link as Pull Ref, only initiated from the source side. ‹src: discussion-reference-continuity›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-171 | CONT | idea | Continuity links | Both link kinds sit under one "continuity link" concept, with differently colored badges (for example, purple or violet for reference, blue or amber for match). A target frame with a reference link shows a badge in a different color from match badges. ‹src: discussion-reference-continuity›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-172 | CONT | idea | Continuity links | A frame can hold several reference kinds at once: continuity references, character references, and style references. The continuity reference must be separable, so it can be added, replaced, or removed without disturbing the others. ‹src: discussion-reference-continuity›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-173 | CONT | idea | Continuity links | Open question: what happens when a reference link breaks? The break behavior still needs to be defined. ‹src: discussion-reference-continuity›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-174 | CONT | impl | Continuity links | A "Use current image as reference" toggle appears at every regeneration point: upload review, scene asset image, transformation post-image, and frame regeneration. When on, the current image guides the style and composition of the new generation to maintain visual consistency. When off, generation works from the description only. ‹src: EnhancedUploadModal-1›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-175 | CONT | impl | Continuity links | The user can pick a reusable "continuity base frame" from frames already generated, as the anchor for a new frame, and review each shot's continuity links. ‹src: current-app-user-flow›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-176 | CONT | alt | Continuity links › Options: link exclusivity | Option A: at most one continuity link per target frame. Between the same pair of frames, match and reference are mutually exclusive, so creating one replaces the other. ‹src: discussion-reference-continuity›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-177 | CONT | alt | Continuity links › Options: link exclusivity | Option B: one link per type per target, for example a match from one neighbor plus a reference from another, shown as multiple badges. Between the same pair of frames, match and reference remain mutually exclusive. Its other branch is WD-IDEA-CONT-176; non-adjacent many-to-many links are WD-IDEA-CONT-184. ‹src: discussion-reference-continuity›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-178 | CONT | alt | Continuity links › Options: stale reference after the source frame is regenerated | Stale reference problem: if the source frame is regenerated after a reference is pulled, a stored copy of its image goes stale. Option A (favored): store the source frame's identity and resolve its current image at generation time, so references always use the latest image. ‹src: discussion-reference-continuity›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-179 | CONT | alt | Continuity links › Options: stale reference after the source frame is regenerated | Option B: keep the image captured at pull time and accept staleness, since the user chose non-reactive behavior. A reference then keeps the old image even after the source frame is regenerated. ‹src: discussion-reference-continuity›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-180 | CONT | planned | Non-adjacent continuity | Continuity cannot be strictly sequential. In shot-reverse-shot, shots 1, 3, and 5 share one setup and shots 2, 4, and 6 share another, so shot 3 needs shot 1's output as its reference, not shot 2's. ‹src: 3.7-phase-5-non-adjacent-continuity›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-181 | CONT | voice,planned | Non-adjacent continuity | Users can manually link non-adjacent frames (for example shot 1B and shot 1E) as continuity references, enabling shot-reverse-shot where shots 1, 3, and 5 share one camera position and shots 2, 4, and 6 share another. ‹src: 3.8-phase5-non-adjacent-continuity, 3.7-tasks-part-2(3.8)›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-182 | CONT | planned | Non-adjacent continuity | A "Link to different shot" picker lists every shot in the scene with start and end frame thumbnails. The linked shot's end frame becomes this shot's continuity reference, and match mode then copies the linked shot's end frame. ‹src: 3.7-phase-5-non-adjacent-continuity›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-183 | CONT | planned | Non-adjacent continuity | A "Link Reference Frame" picker shows thumbnails of all generated frames in the scene, optionally including frames from adjacent or other scenes. Linked frames appear as small removable thumbnails under the current frame. ‹src: 3.8-phase5-non-adjacent-continuity›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-184 | CONT | planned | Non-adjacent continuity | Continuity links are many-to-many. A frame may have several incoming reference links, but duplicate links between the same pair of frames are not allowed. Shots without an explicit link fall back to the adjacent shot, preserving existing behavior. ‹src: 3.7-phase-5-non-adjacent-continuity, 3.8-phase5-non-adjacent-continuity, 3.7-tasks-part-2(3.8)›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-185 | CONT | planned | Non-adjacent continuity | When a frame has multiple references, the user designates one as primary (with a star or badge). The primary gets full image analysis, while secondary references are attached as extra reference images without analysis, keeping cost reasonable. ‹src: 3.8-phase5-non-adjacent-continuity›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-186 | CONT | future | Non-adjacent continuity | Auto-detect alternating camera setups (A-B-A-B) and suggest linking, for example "Shots 1, 3, 5 share camera setup. Link them?", for user confirmation. Nice-to-have. ‹src: 3.7-phase-5-non-adjacent-continuity›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-187 | CONT | planned | Non-adjacent continuity | Visual map of continuity relationships: shots sharing reference links show colored dots or connecting lines in the shot list, with matching groups color-coded (for example, shots 1, 3, 5 blue; shots 2, 4, 6 green). Non-adjacent links appear as colored connector lines, distinct from sequential arrows. ‹src: 3.8-phase5-non-adjacent-continuity, 3.7-phase-5-non-adjacent-continuity›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-188 | CONT | future | Non-adjacent continuity | Non-adjacent links also enable recurring establishing shots, flashback references, and dream-sequence callbacks, including references to arbitrary frames across scenes. ‹src: 3.8-phase5-non-adjacent-continuity›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-189 | CONT | voice | Non-adjacent continuity | Match framing between any two shots, even non-adjacent ones. To reuse a shot's background later in the scene, the user drags that frame in as a reference and marks it "reference only with regard to the background". ‹src: 3.7-location-pre-planning›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-191 | CONT | alt | Non-adjacent continuity › Options: choosing among multiple references (alternatives to a user-starred primary) | Alternative for choosing among multiple references: use the most recent link as the primary. The other alternative is to analyze all linked references and merge their analyses, which costs more. ‹src: 3.8-phase5-non-adjacent-continuity›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-192 | CONT | alt | Non-adjacent continuity › Options: choosing among multiple references (alternatives to a user-starred primary) | Alternative for choosing among multiple references: analyze all linked references and merge their analyses. This is more costly than designating a primary. ‹src: 3.8-phase5-non-adjacent-continuity›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-193 | CONT | alt | Non-adjacent continuity › Options: where alternating-setup detection runs | Option: run alternating-setup detection during prompt generation, which happens earlier and can inform prompts. Placement is unresolved; the alternative is during frame generation, where users already manage references. ‹src: 3.7-phase-5-non-adjacent-continuity›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-194 | CONT | alt | Non-adjacent continuity › Options: where alternating-setup detection runs | Option: run alternating-setup detection during frame generation, where users already manage references. Placement is unresolved; the alternative is during prompt generation, which happens earlier and can inform prompts. ‹src: 3.7-phase-5-non-adjacent-continuity›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-197 | CONT | voice | Frame reuse: approved frames become continuity context | Regenerating every frame from one vague establishing shot risks completely different-looking environments. Preferred workflow: generate one frame using only the establishing reference (regenerating a few times if needed), then reuse and edit that liked frame through the scene and later scenes. The image-level version of this iterate-then-reuse workflow is WD-IDEA-LOC-112; this bullet applies the same workflow to frames. ‹src: post-clean-location-rethink›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-198 | CONT | planned,voice | Frame reuse: approved frames become continuity context | A reused prior frame is editable input for the current frame, not just visual inspiration, and this should be easy and obvious. Users drag a previously generated frame (from any shot, earlier or later, in this or other scenes) into the frame they are working on as a background or composition base, even if it was never designated earlier. They then edit it by prompt, for example a character now holding an object, a new character added, blood on the wall, or the room on fire. Only the delta changes, unedited areas stay the same, and old assets are replaced with new ones rather than generating from a blank canvas. Using the pulled-in frame as-is is expected to be rare; mostly it is a starting point. Drag-and-drop is chosen because the user sees exactly what is pulled in, a source-of-truth starting point that keeps many consistencies even when characters, actions, wardrobe, or body language change. ‹src: 5-1-location-system-intent-source, post-clean-location-rethink, streamlined-input-process-consolidated›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-199 | CONT | planned | Frame reuse: approved frames become continuity context | For each shot, the user chooses one of three paths: reuse or edit an approved frame, generate a variation from a base, or generate fresh. The shot's workspace states its continuity goal (for example, "Preserve kitchen background, change character blocking"), with output actions Generate Variation, Edit From Base, and Generate Fresh Instead. ‹src: 5-3-location-implementation-plan, 5-2-location-product-synthesis›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-200 | CONT | planned,impl | Frame reuse: approved frames become continuity context | The frame stage suggests ranked continuity bases: earlier approved or generated start frames from the same location, each with a reason (same location, same or similar camera direction) and a suitability level, plus an option to generate fresh. Tiers: strong (same location and direction), usable (same location, no direction conflict), weak (same location, different direction). ‹src: 5-3-location-implementation-plan, 7-19-location-stabilization-master-plan, 8-1-location-continuity-phase-5-6-audit›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-201 | CONT | impl | Frame reuse: approved frames become continuity context | Candidate ranking for a continuity base prefers frames from the same scene, then approved frames over merely generated ones, with ties broken by recency. The shot's own frames and frames without images are excluded, and a shot with no linked location gets no candidates. ‹src: 7-19-location-stabilization-master-plan, 8-1-location-continuity-phase-5-6-audit›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-202 | CONT | impl | Frame reuse: approved frames become continuity context | Selecting a continuity base raises the shot's continuity strength. A strong base with no risks yields a "strong" rating. ‹src: 7-19-location-stabilization-master-plan›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-203 | CONT | impl,planned | Frame reuse: approved frames become continuity context | A selected base becomes the primary reference (location views are supporting context only) and switches the shot into reuse/edit mode, overriding match and camera-change modes. The prompt describes only the delta on top of the base, such as "move camera slightly wider," "replace standing pose with seated pose," or "keep same cabinets, oven, doorway layout." The shot's frame prompt gains an explicit reuse/edit instruction block. ‹src: 7-19-location-stabilization-master-plan, 5-2-location-product-synthesis›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-204 | CONT | impl | Frame reuse: approved frames become continuity context | Edge case: if the selected base is later deleted, rejected, or loses its image, generation silently falls back to the shot's normal references instead of failing. A base already in the reference list is not attached twice. ‹src: 7-19-location-stabilization-master-plan, 8-1-location-continuity-phase-5-6-audit›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-205 | CONT | planned | Frame reuse: approved frames become continuity context | The edit-delta workflow clearly separates what should stay the same from what should change, and shows which background and location references are carried into generation. ‹src: 5-3-location-implementation-plan›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-206 | CONT | planned | Frame reuse: approved frames become continuity context | An approved output can stay just an output, be promoted to an established view, or later serve as a continuity base for other shots. ‹src: 5-3-location-implementation-plan›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-209 | CONT | impl,planned | Frame reuse: approved frames become continuity context | Continuity lineage is recorded for each frame: which frame it came from and how, whether by reuse with matching direction, reuse with edit, camera-change reference, match copy, or manual copy. It is shown simply as a chain, e.g. "Shot 12 approved frame → reused for Shot 15 → candidate new reverse anchor," or as current shot, reused approved frame, resulting output, and optional promotion to established view (generated from frame X, promoted from shot Y, established as view Z). Frame lineage here and the view provenance in WD-IDEA-LOC-237 and WD-IDEA-LOC-091 describe the same promotion chain from the frame side and the view side. ‹src: 7-19-location-stabilization-master-plan, 5-2-location-product-synthesis, 5-3-location-implementation-plan›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-210 | CONT | planned | Frame reuse: approved frames become continuity context | The frame stage becomes the main continuity workspace, not just a generation endpoint, and its promise is "where continuity becomes real and reusable." It owns generation output review, frame approval, reuse or editing from existing frames as the default continuity mechanism, establish-as-view actions, and continuity curation across shots. The same role is described in WD-IDEA-FLOW-065 (Stage 10 as the main creative control and continuity workspace). ‹src: 5-1-location-system-intent-source, 5-2-location-product-synthesis›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-211 | CONT | planned | Frame reuse: approved frames become continuity context | Risk: if reuse/edit-from-existing is weak, the product drifts back toward heavy upfront setup. ‹src: 5-3-location-implementation-plan›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-212 | CONT | idea | Continuity review, detection, and repair | Continuity breaks must be detected and flagged to the user, never silently ignored. ‹src: AI-agent-registry-context-flow-architecture›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-213 | CONT | idea | Continuity review, detection, and repair | System prompts and regeneration text boxes are the main practical tools for keeping continuity across scenes. ‹src: Tickets›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-214 | CONT | idea,impl | Continuity review, detection, and repair | Rearview Mirror: the previous scene's ending (final action and dialogue as text, and the final frame), shown for seamless hand-off while the user works on the current scene. Conceived as a standalone collapsible panel at the top of production stages; that panel was judged possibly not useful enough, so the rearview is a tab of the Content Access Space (WD-IDEA-CONT-217, WD-IDEA-UX-160) rather than a standalone panel (see WD-IDEA-CONT-215). ‹src: project-overview, user-flow, development-progress›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-215 | CONT | planned,impl | Continuity review, detection, and repair | The rearview shows the previous scene's rich end-state summary with its final frame thumbnail and flags significant state changes. The Rearview tab presents the immediately preceding scene's end-state summary text and end-frame thumbnail, so the user can keep continuity with where the last scene left off.
- WD-IDEA-CONT-216 | CONT | planned | Continuity review, detection, and repair | When the previous scene is unfinished, the rearview still shows that scene's assets, or a carousel, even though the scene is not finished. ‹src: injecting-org-tickets-into-plan›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-217 | CONT | rejected | Continuity review, detection, and repair | The simple rearview mirror was judged possibly not useful enough and was folded into a multi-tab content panel, the Content Access Space, with rearview, current scene script, and shot list tabs across production stages. The review also asked whether style capsules work well enough. ‹src: Tickets, master-mvp-tasklist›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-218 | CONT | idea | Continuity review, detection, and repair | In frame generation the rearview shows the prior shot's final frame next to the new start frame, with ghost (overlay) and flicker comparison modes. ‹src: project-overview›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-219 | CONT | future,voice | Continuity review, detection, and repair | Rearview comparison modes: ghost overlay, a flicker toggle switching rapidly between the previous end frame and the current start frame, side by side with alignment guides, and a slider, with a mode switcher. Multi-mode comparison of the current shot against previous content is deferred to a final nice-to-have phase (ticket 12.3).
- WD-IDEA-CONT-220 | CONT | idea,impl | Continuity review, detection, and repair | Continuity risk analysis is advisory: it compares the prior scene's end state with the current scene's opening requirements, rates each as Safe / Risky / Broken, and only warns, never edits or blocks. It flags scenes whose dependencies make them risky, such as later scenes that depend on an earlier scene's end state. The scene hub shows each scene's slug and summary; first scenes show no dependency warnings, later scenes show continuity warnings tied to earlier scenes' end states, and audio-driven projects also show the audio timestamps and transcript for each scene. The scene hub display of these per-scene warnings is WD-IDEA-FLOW-036. ‹src: project-overview, development-progress, golden-test-evaluation-framework›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-221 | CONT | planned | Continuity review, detection, and repair | Content-aware risk analysis based on actual end-state summaries flags character, location, prop, timeline, and logic inconsistencies, with specific warnings and suggested corrections. It compares the previous scene's end state with what the current scene expects, and pairs this with visual frame-based consistency tracking.
- WD-IDEA-CONT-222 | CONT | planned | Continuity review, detection, and repair | Continuity override: the user can "Acknowledge and Proceed" past a risky continuity warning, or "Mark as Reviewed" to confirm an intentional break, with the reason stored as an audit trail. ‹src: master-mvp-tasklist, MVP-tasklist-v1.1›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-223 | CONT | planned | Continuity review, detection, and repair | Break detection on edits: removing an asset that later scenes depend on, or changing a state so a prior scene's end state no longer matches the next scene, triggers warnings. ‹src: MVP-tasklist-v1.1›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-224 | CONT | idea | Continuity review, detection, and repair | Continuity drift detector: after the user approves frames, AI compares consecutive frames for character appearance (clothing, hair, face), setting elements (furniture, lighting, background), and props (presence, position), and flags visual inconsistencies between generated frames. ‹src: project-overview, README, AI-agent-registry-context-flow-architecture›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-225 | CONT | idea | Continuity review, detection, and repair | The drift detector tolerates minor variation (lighting shifts, small position changes) and flags major breaks. It rates continuity as safe, risky, or broken, and lists each issue by type (character appearance, missing prop, setting mismatch, spatial inconsistency) and severity (critical, moderate, minor). Each issue gets a concrete suggested fix (e.g. "regenerate frame with explicit shirt color"). ‹src: AI-agent-registry-context-flow-architecture›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-226 | CONT | future | Continuity review, detection, and repair | Drift check between the previous end frame (of a shot or scene) and the current start frame, flagging likely breaks with confidence scores and showing suggestions next to a visual comparison. Continuity drift detection is also floated for final video review (ticket 12.2); one plan deferred it to a final nice-to-have phase.
- WD-IDEA-CONT-227 | CONT | planned | Continuity review, detection, and repair | Visual continuity tracking compares final frames across scenes for visual drift in character appearance, props, and locations, and tracks how each evolves. ‹src: master-mvp-tasklist, MVP-tasklist-v1.1›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-228 | CONT | future | Continuity review, detection, and repair | Automated continuity analysis: visual similarity scoring, character appearance tracking, prop position validation, lighting and atmosphere consistency checks, automatic flags, and correction suggestions. ‹src: Post-MVP-tasklist, MVP-tasklist-v1.1›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-229 | CONT | planned | Continuity review, detection, and repair | Project-level continuity dashboard: a single overview of the continuity status of every scene, highlighting problem areas and tracking resolution progress, plus warnings flagging timeline and logic problems. ‹src: master-mvp-tasklist, MVP-tasklist-v1.1›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-230 | CONT | future | Continuity review, detection, and repair | Exportable continuity reports, continuity notes for production teams, and visual continuity reference sheets. ‹src: MVP-tasklist-v1.1›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-231 | CONT | future | Continuity review, detection, and repair | Advanced continuity tools are a post-MVP professional feature. ‹src: master-mvp-tasklist›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-232 | CONT | idea | Continuity review, detection, and repair | Generated anchor frames go through continuity validation. A frame that fails can be regenerated, or fixed by region inpainting: the user selects a region such as a face and gives a local instruction (e.g. "change expression from surprise to neutral") to fix the break without regenerating the whole frame. ‹src: architecture-and-rules, database-schema-state-transition-matrix, project-overview›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-233 | CONT | impl | Continuity review, detection, and repair | The prompt stage shows a continuity preview and per-shot asset timelines, can generate continuity prompt text, and offers repair controls that send the user back to the scene visuals stage (Stage 8) to fix assets, missing visuals, or transformations, then forward again to regenerate prompts. ‹src: current-app-user-flow›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-234 | CONT | idea | When upstream things change | When an upstream stage is edited, every downstream AI step re-runs with freshly assembled context carrying the edited content and its new version number. Unchanged inputs (project parameters, style capsule, global context, previous frame reference) are reused as-is. ‹src: AI-agent-registry-context-flow-architecture›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-235 | CONT | idea | When upstream things change | The end-frame anchor chain makes continuity cascade. A script or production edit in scene N, or regenerating a start or end frame there, marks scene N's video outdated, updates its end frame, and marks every later scene "continuity broken." Editing scene N's shot list after frames or video exist marks its visual, prompt, and video stages (Stages 8-12) outdated and every later scene "continuity broken," and recalculates scene N's frame costs and warns about the downstream impact. This is a high-cost cascade. Invalidation-scope alternatives are WD-IDEA-UX-063 to WD-IDEA-UX-067; this bullet is the high-cost option described in WD-IDEA-UX-066. ‹src: project-overview, database-schema-state-transition-matrix, AI-agent-registry-context-flow-architecture›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-238 | CONT | impl | When upstream things change | When the master script is re-approved after scene work exists, the system tries to keep stable scene identities so existing scene work survives. Scenes that were removed become "continuity broken" instead of silently disappearing. ‹src: current-app-user-flow›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-239 | CONT | voice | When upstream things change | Scenario to support: Phase A yields 9 scenes and scenes 1–3 are fully produced, and the user dislikes the middle scenes (3–5), the ending (7–9), or just scenes 4 and 9. They must be able to pivot without losing anything made for scenes 1–3, and must never lose access to global asset generations or any frames, assets, and videos already made. The scene-preserving pivot interaction is WD-IDEA-UX-092 to WD-IDEA-UX-094. ‹src: Section-of-story-branch›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-240 | CONT | future | When upstream things change | "Update existing project from new script": push a second draft into the pipeline, diff it against the existing script, flag changed scenes, preserve stable scenes' downstream work, and send only changed scenes back to shot-list re-entry. Script revision reconciliation follows the same approach: push a second draft without losing downstream work, diff new against old, flag which scenes changed, preserve untouched scenes, and mark only changed scenes for re-entry at the shot-list stage (Stage 7), framed as a controlled update, not a fork. ‹src: streamlined-input-analysis-5-10, streamline-master-plan-8-1, streamline-plan-critique-8-1›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-241 | CONT | future | When upstream things change | "Remix mode" (revision sense): after a project is complete, upload a revised script and get an automated diff flagging which scenes need regeneration, while unaffected scene work stays intact. ‹src: streamlined-input-analysis-5-10›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-242 | CONT | future | When upstream things change | A script revision can invalidate a scattered, non-contiguous set of scenes across Stages 7–12 at once. This is a different staleness shape from forward-only stage invalidation, and its deliberate re-sync stance must be reconciled with backfill's no-re-sync stance (WD-IDEA-FLOW-089). ‹src: streamline-plan-critique-8-1, streamline-implementation-spec-8-3›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-243 | CONT | future,alt | When upstream things change › Options: how a script revision is reconciled | Script revision reconciliation is structurally branch work, with two alternatives. Option one: fork a new branch at the revision point, which preserves prior state but leaves two branches to reconcile. ‹src: streamline-implementation-spec-8-3›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-244 | CONT | future,alt | When upstream things change › Options: how a script revision is reconciled | Script revision reconciliation is structurally branch work, with two alternatives. Option two: mutate the active branch guided by a script diff, which gives a cleaner mental model but needs real rollback. ‹src: streamline-implementation-spec-8-3›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-245 | CONT | future,alt | When upstream things change › Options: defining "a scene changed" | Defining "a scene changed" for revision has competing options. Option one: raw text diff, which is fast but noisy. ‹src: streamline-implementation-spec-8-3›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-246 | CONT | future,alt | When upstream things change › Options: defining "a scene changed" | Defining "a scene changed" for revision has competing options. Option two: semantic AI diff, which is expensive. ‹src: streamline-implementation-spec-8-3›
- WD-IDEA-CONT-247 | CONT | future,alt | When upstream things change › Options: defining "a scene changed" | Defining "a scene changed" for revision has competing options. Option three: manual per-scene confirmation, which is cheap but slow for long scripts. ‹src: streamline-implementation-spec-8-3›

## 05 Writing & Story System (05-WRITING-AND-STORY.md)

- WD-IDEA-STORY-001 | STORY | impl | Narrative chain and shared context | Each writing stage reads only its upstream: treatment from the processed Stage 1 input; beats from the selected treatment alone (unselected variants never flow on); script from beats (and treatment, see Options below); assets from the script's scenes.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-002 | STORY | impl | Narrative chain and shared context | Every AI writing step is conditioned on the same project parameters: target screen-time range in seconds, project type (narrative, commercial, audio-visual), content rating (G/PG/PG-13/M), genres, free-text tonal guidance, plus the writing style capsule. Treatment also sees the story idea and optional uploaded context files. Rating sets baseline limits on dialogue and action; target length makes generation adjust narrative density to fit the time.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-003 | STORY | - | Narrative chain and shared context | Narrative stages inherit all global intent: they may refine the global tone but never contradict it. ‹src: inheritance_contract›
- WD-IDEA-STORY-004 | STORY | impl | Narrative chain and shared context | Visual storytelling is preferred throughout: treatments favor visual storytelling over dialogue-heavy sequences, and scripts are visually rich because later stages generate images from them. ‹src: system-prompts-inventory›
- WD-IDEA-STORY-005 | STORY | - | Treatment (Stage 2) | Treatment is continuous high-level prose with no scene breaks or beats; scene structure is introduced later.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-006 | STORY | impl | Treatment (Stage 2) | AI generates exactly three treatment variants at once, each structurally different (different ending, chronology, or tonal emphasis), each labeled with its approach (e.g. "dark ending", "redemption arc") and an estimated runtime so the user can compare them.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-007 | STORY | impl | Treatment (Stage 2) | Each variant is a complete 3-act story arc sized to the target runtime, roughly 800-1200 words.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-008 | STORY | - | Treatment (Stage 2) | Quality expectation: clear character arcs; dialogue hinted at but not fully scripted; emphasis matched to genre (spatial relationships and timing for action, clearly defined character roles). ‹src: golden-test-evaluation-framework›
- WD-IDEA-STORY-009 | STORY | impl | Treatment (Stage 2) | Treatments generate automatically on first visit, with a manual "Generate Treatments" fallback when none exist.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-010 | STORY | - | Treatment (Stage 2) | The user must pick one variant to proceed; the choice is recorded. Legacy docs describe a variation gallery (cards) for switching the active variant later, updating the current branch.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-011 | STORY | impl | Treatment (Stage 2) | The user can generate an additional variation on demand. ‹src: current-app-user-flow›
- WD-IDEA-STORY-012 | STORY | impl | Treatment (Stage 2) | Treatments are directly editable as rich text in an edit mode with explicit save; switching variants with unsaved edits asks for confirmation.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-013 | STORY | impl | Treatment (Stage 2) | Full treatment regeneration with guidance replaces all three variants, keeping no history. ‹src: planning-wif-lawrence›
- WD-IDEA-STORY-014 | STORY | impl | Treatment (Stage 2) | Known limitation: no way to paste or import one's own treatment, and manual edits stay attributed to the AI variant, so a user-written version cannot be kept as the "original". ‹src: planning-wif-lawrence›
- WD-IDEA-STORY-015 | STORY | - | Beat sheet (Stage 3) | A beat is one atomic plot event or emotional shift that advances the story: self-contained, action-oriented (what happens, not mood), linked by cause and effect to its neighbors.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-016 | STORY | impl | Beat sheet (Stage 3) | Beat extraction targets 15-30 beats of 1-3 sentences, in a numbered list, chronological unless non-linear storytelling is intended, together covering the full arc.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-017 | STORY | - | Beat sheet (Stage 3) | Quality expectation: beat count scales with complexity (about 8-12 for a simple scene, 15-25 for an action sequence, 20-30 for a 15-minute podcast); beats follow setup → rising tension → resolution, with specific physical actions, timing relationships, and escalating stakes. ‹src: golden-test-evaluation-framework›
- WD-IDEA-STORY-018 | STORY | impl | Beat sheet (Stage 3) | Each beat carries a rationale for why it is structural, an estimated screen time derived from target length, and the treatment excerpt it came from; the sheet reports total estimated runtime and a named structure (3-act, hero's journey, non-linear).
- WD-IDEA-STORY-019 | STORY | impl | Beat sheet (Stage 3) | Each beat records whether the user edited it by hand.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-020 | STORY | impl | Beat sheet (Stage 3) | Beats generate automatically on first visit, with a manual fallback; generation must visibly produce output (an early "does nothing" failure was a ticket).
- WD-IDEA-STORY-021 | STORY | impl | Beat sheet (Stage 3) | Beat sheet generation may produce several variations (e.g. three); status messages must match what is actually produced and shown. ‹src: Tickets, master-mvp-tasklist›
- WD-IDEA-STORY-022 | STORY | impl | Beat sheet (Stage 3) | Drag-and-drop reordering to experiment with chronology; numbers update instantly. Drag-and-drop is part of the general interaction toolkit (beats, shots).
- WD-IDEA-STORY-023 | STORY | impl | Beat sheet (Stage 3) | Each beat is a click-to-edit field that also supports targeted regeneration of part or all of the beat (e.g. "make this beat more dramatic").
- WD-IDEA-STORY-024 | STORY | - | Beat sheet (Stage 3) | Adding a beat opens the guidance box; AI writes the new beat from the guidance using adjacent beats as context. In the built form, "add after any beat" inserts a placeholder ("New beat — describe what happens here...").
- WD-IDEA-STORY-025 | STORY | - | Beat sheet (Stage 3) | Removing a beat is a plain deletion; the next beat becomes the continuity anchor.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-026 | STORY | impl | Beat sheet (Stage 3) | Beat context view: each beat expands (or opens a modal) to show the exact treatment prose it came from, giving traceability from structure back to prose.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-027 | STORY | impl | Beat sheet (Stage 3) | Brainstorm alternatives for one beat (e.g. "3 alternative resolutions for Beat 10"): three versions that keep the same narrative function and screen time and stay consistent with surrounding beats while varying tone, action, or character focus; the user picks one to replace the original.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-028 | STORY | impl | Beat sheet (Stage 3) | Split/expand a beat into 2-3 sub-beats, optionally guided; total screen time and cause-and-effect flow are preserved.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-029 | STORY | impl | Beat sheet (Stage 3) | Full beat-sheet regeneration requires written guidance (min 10 characters) and a treatment source.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-030 | STORY | impl | Beat sheet (Stage 3) | Confirmation rules: at least 3 beats, each at least 10 characters.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-032 | STORY | voice | Beat sheet (Stage 3) | Beat durations must be editable: new beats default to 20 seconds, which is wrong for short moments, so offer a dial to nudge length or direct time entry. ‹src: new-tasks›
- WD-IDEA-STORY-033 | STORY | voice | Beat sheet (Stage 3) | Projected runtime should be visible so the user can track ballooning (e.g. a 2-3 minute project producing 6 scenes of 6-10 eight-second shots). Placement options: ‹src: new-tasks› / sum of beat durations on the beat sheet; / projected runtime in the Script Hub; / running tally of all planned shots and durations in the shot list.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-034 | STORY | impl | Master script (Stage 4) › What the script is | The master script is a "visually verbose blueprint" (the "Verbosity Engine"), not a concise theatrical script: AI maximizes description so every element can become an image. It covers: / character appearance, clothing, posture, expressions, body language; / setting, lighting, atmosphere, mood, spatial layout; / props and their visual significance; / camera-ready action and camera implications (no technical specs yet); / choreography of every movement and gesture; emotional subtext through visual cues.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-035 | STORY | - | Master script (Stage 4) › What the script is | Standard screenplays are concise, flowery, and low on description (no character positioning, attributes, detailed settings, or clear action), so production prompting needs explicit descriptive detail beyond screenplay convention. ‹src: Tickets›
- WD-IDEA-STORY-036 | STORY | impl | Master script (Stage 4) › What the script is | Industry-standard format: ALL CAPS scene headings (INT./EXT. LOCATION - TIME), character names, and SFX; character names in caps before dialogue; present-tense action. Every scene opens with a proper, unique heading, which is what later makes scene and location splitting possible.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-037 | STORY | - | Master script (Stage 4) › What the script is | Dialogue must be character-specific and purposeful; all dialogue and action must serve the emotional and structural goals of the corresponding beat.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-038 | STORY | - | Master script (Stage 4) › What the script is | Quality expectation: names in caps before dialogue; action rich in posture, expression, and setting detail written for image/video generation; natural, personality-revealing dialogue; SFX cues for action; length matched to target (e.g. 2-3 pages). ‹src: golden-test-evaluation-framework›
- WD-IDEA-STORY-039 | STORY | - | Master script (Stage 4) › What the script is | Every beat becomes one or more scenes; each scene records which beats it covers, which characters, props, and settings it mentions, and its estimated duration.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-040 | STORY | alt | Master script (Stage 4) › Options: what context script generation reads | Full treatment as context while adhering to the beat sheet; script must consume both coherently so a beat edit that drifted from the treatment does not confuse it.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-041 | STORY | alt | Master script (Stage 4) › Options: what context script generation reads | Beat sheet, rating, genre, tonal guidance, and capsule only; treatment prose is not seen directly. ‹src: AI-agent-registry-context-flow-architecture›
- WD-IDEA-STORY-042 | STORY | voice,impl | Master script (Stage 4) › Spatial precision in the script | Scene headings name the specific sub-location, not just the building: "INT. GINGERBREAD HOUSE - KITCHEN - DAY", not "INT. GINGERBREAD HOUSE"; generation is instructed to produce room-level precision so each heading anchors one space (see 06B). ‹src: 3.7-location-pre-planning, 3.7-location-system-enhancement-(master-doc)›
- WD-IDEA-STORY-043 | STORY | impl,voice | Master script (Stage 4) › Spatial precision in the script | Action lines are spatially explicit: every action anchors characters to positions and states where they move ("They argue across the kitchen counter. He storms out through the hallway toward the front door", "She crosses to the window"), not just mood. This applies to subtle moves in otherwise static scenes ("walks to the corner of the room"). Feeds shot extraction (see 07).
- WD-IDEA-STORY-044 | STORY | alt,voice | Master script (Stage 4) › Options: how mid-scene location changes are written | Flag location-dynamic scenes in the slug; action text explicitly describes every setting change, major or subtle ("they walk into the kitchen"), and that description persists so Stage 7 shot extraction catches it. Shot extraction may also use smart detection of in-scene setting changes, complementing the slug flag or the heading split in WD-IDEA-STORY-045. ‹src: 3.7-location-pre-planning›
- WD-IDEA-STORY-045 | STORY | alt,impl | Master script (Stage 4) › Options: how mid-scene location changes are written | Split into separate headings joined by CONTINUOUS so each heading has exactly one sub-location while narrative flow continues; precise headings plus explicit action suffice, no special "dynamic scene" flag. ‹src: 3.7-location-system-enhancement-(master-doc)›
- WD-IDEA-STORY-046 | STORY | impl,planned | Master script (Stage 4) › Editor and workflow | The script auto-generates on first entry if none exists (checks for an existing script, shows loading, falls back if generation fails) so the user never lands on an empty stage; a manual "Generate Master Script" button remains.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-047 | STORY | impl | Master script (Stage 4) › Editor and workflow | The screenplay editor understands screenplay elements (scene heading, action, character, dialogue, parenthetical, transition), has a formatting toolbar, and renders industry-standard layout (indented dialogue, consistent spacing); improved character/dialogue handling was a recurring polish goal.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-048 | STORY | impl | Master script (Stage 4) › Editor and workflow | Pasted screenplay text is accepted, but there is no one-click file import; a file import was proposed (see 02B).
- WD-IDEA-STORY-049 | STORY | impl | Master script (Stage 4) › Editor and workflow | Beat alignment panel: a collapsible, read-only sidebar of beats that highlights the beat matching the visible script section; clicking a beat scrolls to it.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-050 | STORY | impl | Master script (Stage 4) › Editor and workflow | Before approval, the user previews the scenes that will be extracted; planned: the preview also shows each scene's characters, location, and props before commit.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-051 | STORY | impl | Master script (Stage 4) › Editor and workflow | Approval rules: script not empty, contains INT./EXT. headings, every extracted scene has content. Related: WD-IDEA-INPUT-102 (an import with zero extracted scenes must not claim success).
- WD-IDEA-STORY-052 | STORY | future | Master script (Stage 4) › Editor and workflow | Markdown support in editors and syntax highlighting for scripts.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-053 | STORY | impl | Master script (Stage 4) › Script as structured document | The script is stored as typed blocks (scene heading, action, dialogue line, transition, paragraph), so the block type is the classification, not a guess from text.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-054 | STORY | impl | Master script (Stage 4) › Script as structured document | A dialogue line is one block in a fixed inline form: CHARACTER NAME, optional extensions (V.O., O.S., CONT'D), colon, optional parenthetical delivery note, then the quoted line.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-055 | STORY | - | Master script (Stage 4) › Script as structured document | Round-tripping a screenplay between plain text and the structured editor must preserve headings, character/dialogue blocks, and parentheticals without loss.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-056 | STORY | - | Master script (Stage 4) › Script as structured document | Legacy docs describe older plain-text scripts converting to the structured form on load and saving in it thereafter, with scenes that have missing dependencies re-extractable once.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-058 | STORY | impl | Scene breakdown (script → scenes) | Each scene gets a readable slug derived from its heading (e.g. "INT. KITCHEN - DAY").
- WD-IDEA-STORY-059 | STORY | impl | Scene breakdown (script → scenes) | Every scene heading marks a boundary and yields INT/EXT, location, and time of day. Time is recognized from known trailing tokens (DAY, NIGHT, CONTINUOUS, LATER, MOMENTS LATER, DAWN, DUSK, MORNING, AFTERNOON, EVENING); everything between is the compound location (building + sub-location). Headings without a time token and old simple headings still parse; identical compound locations dedupe across scenes. Location sets the scene's expected location; INT/EXT and time feed frame context.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-060 | STORY | impl | Scene breakdown (script → scenes) | Character names with extensions such as (V.O.) count as the same character; empty scripts are handled gracefully.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-061 | STORY | impl | Scene breakdown (script → scenes) | One unified script manifest serves every downstream stage instead of separate extractions at Stages 5, 6, and 8: per scene (heading, INT/EXT, location, time, present and speaking characters, props, ordered dialogue, combined action text) plus global deduplicated characters, locations, and props.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-062 | STORY | idea | Scene breakdown (script → scenes) | Dialogue is captured complete, attributed, extension-aware, mapped to scene, ordered, with parentheticals ("whispering", "angry") kept as delivery context for voice generation (see 09). ‹src: tiptap-json-extraction-guide›
- WD-IDEA-STORY-063 | STORY | alt | Scene breakdown (script → scenes) › Options: how scene dependencies are extracted | AI pass that pulls each scene's character names, location, and key props from the script. Not the primary method; kept only as a fallback for legacy or unstructured scripts.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-064 | STORY | impl | Scene breakdown (script → scenes) › Options: how scene dependencies are extracted | Deterministic parsing of the structured script (repeatable, fast, cheap), avoiding misclassification such as all-caps action ("THE BUILDING EXPLODES") read as a character; AI extraction is the fallback.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-065 | STORY | future | Scene breakdown (script → scenes) › Options: where descriptive production detail lives (the "Master Document") | A separate Master Document stage between Stages 4 and 5 turns the base script into a super-descriptive source of truth describing every scene and moment of change, including dialogue moments, with full character, wardrobe, prop, and setting descriptions (e.g. Persephone's glow, smile, tunic, flower crown, berry basket, forest). Deferred past MVP; pipeline stays at 12 stages; prompts pulling rich context from earlier stages stand in for it. / Process: finalize script, check inputs for character, asset, location, and action descriptions, then generate the document from the base script.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-069 | STORY | impl | Partial rewrites, alternatives, and regeneration | Targeted regeneration: highlight any sentence, paragraph, or several paragraphs, open an inline box (right-click / beside the selection), type a custom instruction ("inject more conflict", "make sure the walls are peeling and green"); AI rewrites only the selection using surrounding text as context. Available on treatment, beats, script, and asset descriptions; clearly signposted; the user previews before accepting.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-070 | STORY | impl | Partial rewrites, alternatives, and regeneration | Same pattern in Stages 2 and 4: "Edit Selection" returns either one guided rewrite or three alternatives to choose from (script selections require guidance of at least 10 characters).
- WD-IDEA-STORY-071 | STORY | impl | Partial rewrites, alternatives, and regeneration | Partial rewrite rule: only the selected text may change; surrounding text is read-only context; the rewrite matches surrounding tone and style and reads naturally in place.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-072 | STORY | impl | Partial rewrites, alternatives, and regeneration | The three alternatives must each take a distinctly different creative approach; any guidance applies to all three.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-073 | STORY | impl | Partial rewrites, alternatives, and regeneration | Script rewrites and alternatives keep screenplay format, the original's narrative function and timing, and visual verbosity.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-074 | STORY | impl | Partial rewrites, alternatives, and regeneration | Every regeneration stores the user's free-text "why" with the new version, and that guidance shapes the output. It covers treatment, beats, script, assets, and scene assets, and is also stored as that version's commit message.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-075 | STORY | planned | Partial rewrites, alternatives, and regeneration | Regeneration controls say plainly what will happen, offer confirmation or preview first, and behave identically across stages (notably Stage 4 and the pipeline graphic).
- WD-IDEA-STORY-076 | STORY | planned | Partial rewrites, alternatives, and regeneration | Regeneration polish: before/after comparison where it applies, progress indicators, cost shown before regenerating, consistent controls across Stages 2-4. Related: WD-IDEA-UX-070 (cost-aware warning).
- WD-IDEA-STORY-077 | STORY | alt | Partial rewrites, alternatives, and regeneration › Options: may full regeneration be blind? | Full regeneration of treatment, beats, or script is always directed: gated by the mandatory "Why" box (min 10 characters). Legacy app-flow docs describe this as built behavior for Stage 2 treatments; it competes with the blind option in WD-IDEA-STORY-078. ‹src: project-overview›
- WD-IDEA-STORY-078 | STORY | alt,impl | Partial rewrites, alternatives, and regeneration › Options: may full regeneration be blind? | Contextual regeneration: regenerate opens an input for what should change, but a "blind" regeneration option remains, in Stages 2, 3 and wherever regeneration exists. ‹src: Tickets›
- WD-IDEA-STORY-079 | STORY | - | How user edits shape later AI calls | Manual edits immediately override AI text and are locked and carried forward as input to the next stage.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-080 | STORY | - | How user edits shape later AI calls | When regenerating a script from edited beats, AI may optionally receive the previous script version for comparison and guidance.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-082 | STORY | planned | How user edits shape later AI calls | Reconciliation pass: after clarification Q&A, AI applies answers to the user's own submitted documents with variable edit scope (see 02B): / word patch: rename "Marc"→"Marcus" everywhere, near-free find/replace; / clause insertion or paragraph rewrite: "the accent is Irish" inserted into one description; / structural cascade: a plot answer re-splits or rewrites sections across beat sheet and script.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-083 | STORY | planned | How user edits shape later AI calls | Reconciliation runs once per clarification session across all submitted documents and the full Q&A transcript, identifying every affected document.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-084 | STORY | planned | How user edits shape later AI calls | No silent rewrites: each affected document gets a one-line diff summary, and the review screen shows "N edits applied from your answers", separate from stage provenance badges.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-085 | STORY | planned | How user edits shape later AI calls | A plot-affecting (structural cascade) answer gets its own inline confirmation at answer time ("applying it will rewrite two scenes in your beat sheet and script — proceed?"). Cancelling leaves the document unchanged and leaves no record claiming the edit was applied.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-086 | STORY | impl | Writing style capsules | A writing style capsule is a user- or system-defined package of example excerpts, optional labels/descriptors (minimalist, ornate, detached, noir, terse), negative constraints (what to avoid), and freeform notes, stored as plain text and injected into prompts. It steers tone, vocabulary, pacing, dialogue delivery, and descriptive style by explicit imitation, not similarity search.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-087 | STORY | - | Writing style capsules | Capsule selection happens at Stage 1 from system presets, the personal library, or created inline; it is clearly optional, and without one AI defaults to a neutral, descriptive prose style (see WD-IDEA-INPUT-016). ‹src: project-overview›
- WD-IDEA-STORY-088 | STORY | planned,voice | Writing style capsules | Generation-quality effort: an evaluation framework for rating output, A/B tests of prompting strategies for dialogue and narrative and of system-prompt quality, measurement of how much capsules actually change output, and documentation of what works so the rest can be dropped.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-089 | STORY | planned | Writing style capsules | The writing AI should handle raunchy dialogue and violent or spicy mature content (nothing illegal), with workarounds for provider censorship.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-090 | STORY | future | Writing style capsules | Dialogue style training: the user uploads a corpus of their writing, a text model is fine-tuned to their dialogue style, and the custom model is selectable and comparable in the pipeline.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-091 | STORY | alt | Writing style capsules › Options: where the writing capsule is injected | Across all writing steps: Stages 1-4, 7, and 9 (treatment, beats, script, and later text steps).
- WD-IDEA-STORY-092 | STORY | alt,planned | Writing style capsules › Options: where the writing capsule is injected | Focus on, or limit to, script writing and dialogue, because LLMs are weakest at dialogue; make capsules more effective there.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-093 | STORY | - | Audio-driven projects | For audio-driven projects, treatments follow the audio's timestamps and topics, suggest visual metaphors for abstract concepts, and complement rather than compete with the audio. Related: WD-IDEA-INPUT-009. ‹src: golden-test-evaluation-framework›
- WD-IDEA-STORY-094 | STORY | - | Audio-driven projects | Their beats align with audio topic transitions, cite timestamps, and each has a clear visual goal (charts, demonstrations, interview shots).
- WD-IDEA-STORY-095 | STORY | - | Audio-driven projects | Their "script" becomes a series of visual scenes timed to the audio, with timing cues tied to timestamps and transitions on topic changes.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-096 | STORY | - | Imported material and skipped stages | Stage 2 routing: a supplied treatment imports as Stage 2 content (as-is or AI-formatted); notes or long prose generate a treatment; a finished script lets Stage 2 be skipped or optionally backfilled.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-097 | STORY | - | Imported material and skipped stages | Stage 3 routing: a supplied beat sheet is parsed into structured beats; a treatment generates beats normally; a finished script lets Stage 3 be skipped or optionally reverse-generated.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-098 | STORY | - | Imported material and skipped stages | Stage 4 routing: a formatted screenplay imports directly with scenes parsed deterministically (Script Skip mode for prepared users); otherwise the script generates from treatment and beats (see 02B).
- WD-IDEA-STORY-100 | STORY | - | Imported material and skipped stages | Open question: is an imported script the source of truth, or may treatment/beat backfills affect it?
- WD-IDEA-STORY-101 | STORY | impl | Keeping treatment, beats, and script in sync | Each side shows its own flag: the beat sheet (Stage 3) shows "Not Up-to-Date with Current Script" and the master script (Stage 4) shows "Not Up-to-Date with Current Beat Sheet" (see WD-IDEA-STORY-102 and WD-IDEA-UX-050), so drift is visible in both directions.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-102 | STORY | - | Keeping treatment, beats, and script in sync | Editing beats after the script exists sets mutual flags: Stage 3 "Not Up-to-Date with Current Script", Stage 4 "Not Up-to-Date with Current Beat Sheet". Nothing regenerates automatically and no branch is created by the edit itself.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-103 | STORY | - | Keeping treatment, beats, and script in sync | Resync A, "Retroactively Revise Script": regenerate the script from edited beats plus Stage 1-2 context as a new script version; the Stage 3 flag clears. Also one option for beat-sheet/script drift; the reverse direction is WD-IDEA-STORY-104.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-104 | STORY | - | Keeping treatment, beats, and script in sync | Resync B, "Retroactively Revise Beat Sheet (to Sync with Script)": re-extract beats from the current script as source truth (reverse sync), as a new beat-sheet version on the same branch; the flag clears. Also one option for beat-sheet/script drift; the forward direction is WD-IDEA-STORY-103.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-105 | STORY | - | Keeping treatment, beats, and script in sync | Script edits never auto-edit or invalidate the beat sheet; it only shows the passive flag with a manual sync when the user returns.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-106 | STORY | - | Keeping treatment, beats, and script in sync | The system watches manual script edits for structural deviations (e.g. deleting a whole plot-point scene) and raises a non-blocking flag, resolved by revising the beat sheet retroactively.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-107 | STORY | alt | Keeping treatment, beats, and script in sync › Options: how the treatment follows beat-sheet edits | Non-blocking "Not Up-to-Date with Current Beat Sheet" flag on the treatment; "Retroactively Revise Treatment" regenerates the prose from current beats as a new version on the same branch.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-108 | STORY | alt,voice | Keeping treatment, beats, and script in sync › Options: how the treatment follows beat-sheet edits | Treat treatment and beats as codependent: if beats are edited, reactively edit the treatment to include the changes, with a pop-up explaining it; if not, proceed straight to the script. ‹src: new-tasks›
- WD-IDEA-STORY-110 | STORY | - | Story changes downstream (see 10) | Full script regeneration is high-impact because it breaks all Phase B work. Related: WD-IDEA-UX-071 (cost modal wording).
- WD-IDEA-STORY-113 | STORY | impl | Story changes downstream (see 10) | Re-approving the script after scene work exists keeps stable scene identities so existing work survives; removed scenes become "continuity broken" rather than vanishing silently (see 10).
- WD-IDEA-STORY-114 | STORY | voice | Story changes downstream (see 10) | Partial story pivot: when unlocking the script, the user chooses which scenes to keep valid (e.g. finished scenes 1-3 while rewriting 4-5 or the ending). Kept scenes stay frozen and uneditable while the rest is edited and regenerated; on approval they remain unchanged in scene storage and the Script Hub, and new scenes fill the rest of the new branch. ‹src: Section-of-story-branch›
- WD-IDEA-STORY-119 | STORY | future | Future story tools | Beat brainstorming agent for AI-assisted ideation.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-120 | STORY | future | Future story tools | Character development assistant.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-121 | STORY | future | Future story tools | Plot hole detection.
- WD-IDEA-STORY-122 | STORY | future | Future story tools | Narrative structure analyzer.
